<p>President <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a> made a stop over at Clipperton in July 1938 aboard the <a href="/wiki/USS_Houston_(CA-30)" title="USS Houston (CA-30)">USS&#160;<i>Houston</i></a> as part of a fishing expedition to the <a href="/wiki/Gal%C3%A1pagos_Islands" title="Galápagos Islands">Galápagos Islands</a> and other points along the Central and South American coasts.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-USS_Houston_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USS_Houston-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> At the island, Roosevelt and his party spent time fishing for sharks,<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> and afterwards Dr. <a href="/wiki/Waldo_L._Schmitt" title="Waldo L. Schmitt">Waldo L. Schmitt</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution" title="Smithsonian Institution">Smithsonian Institution</a> went ashore with some crew to gather scientific samples and make observations of the island.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Roosevelt had previously tried to visit Clipperton in July 1934 after transiting through the Panama Canal en route to Hawaii on the <i>Houston</i>; he had heard the area was good for fishing, but heavy seas prevented them from lowering a boat when they reached the island.<sup id="cite_ref-USS_Houston_164-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USS_Houston-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> On 19 July 1934, soon after the stop at Clipperton, the rigid airship <a href="/wiki/USS_Macon_(ZRS-5)" title="USS Macon (ZRS-5)">USS&#160;<i>Macon</i></a> rendezvoused with the <i>Houston</i>, and one of the <i>Macon</i><span class="nowrap" style="padding-left:0.1em;">&#39;s</span> <a href="/wiki/Curtiss_F9C_Sparrowhawk" title="Curtiss F9C Sparrowhawk">Curtiss F9C</a> biplanes delivered mail to the president.<sup id="cite_ref-USS_Houston_164-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USS_Houston-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="American_occupation_.281944.E2.80.931945.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="American_occupation_(1944–1945)">American occupation (1944–1945)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clipperton_Island&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: American occupation (1944–1945)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1062260506">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:20%; ;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>The Government of the United States is aware of the extent to which the French Government is desirous to cooperate, in all domains, to the success of the Allied Armies, in Europe as well as in the Pacific. It will understand, however, its concern that French sovereignty be not disregarded in any part of the empire.
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="left-aligned" style="">— <a href="/wiki/Georges_Bidault" title="Georges Bidault">Georges Bidault</a>,&#32;<sup id="cite_ref-DiplomaticPapers1946_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DiplomaticPapers1946-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page: 789">&#58;&#8202;789&#8202;</span></sup></cite>
</p>
</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NH_122194.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/NH_122194.jpg/220px-NH_122194.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="134" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/NH_122194.jpg/330px-NH_122194.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/NH_122194.jpg/440px-NH_122194.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1160" data-file-height="709" /></a><figcaption>The U.S. Navy weather station on the northern side of Clipperton. View is from the top of a radio tower, looking northwest.</figcaption></figure><p>In April 1944, the <a href="/wiki/USS_Atlanta_(CL-51)" title="USS Atlanta (CL-51)">USS&#160;<i>Atlanta</i></a> took observations of Clipperton while en route to Hawaii.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> After an overflight of the island by planes from the <a href="/wiki/USS_Detroit_(CL-8)" title="USS Detroit (CL-8)">USS&#160;<i>Detroit</i></a> and <a href="/wiki/USS_Nevada_(BB-36)" title="USS Nevada (BB-36)">USS&#160;<i>Nevada</i></a> to ensure Clipperton was uninhabited,<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/USS_Argus_(PY-14)" title="USS Argus (PY-14)">USS&#160;<i>Argus</i></a> departed San Francisco on 4 December 1944 with <a href="/wiki/Atmospheric_science" title="Atmospheric science">aerological</a> specialists and personnel and was followed several days later by <a href="/wiki/USS_LST-563" title="USS LST-563">USS&#160;<i>LST-563</i></a> with provisions, heavy equipment, and equipment for construction of a <a href="/wiki/United_States_Navy" title="United States Navy">U.S. Navy</a> weather station on the island.<sup id="cite_ref-Lowry1962_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lowry1962-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Converse2005_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Converse2005-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Thorne1978_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thorne1978-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> The sailors at the weather station were armed in case of a possible Japanese attack in the region.<sup id="cite_ref-DiplomaticPapers1946_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DiplomaticPapers1946-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> Landing on the island proved challenging. <i>LST-563</i> grounded on the reef and the salvage ship <a href="/wiki/USS_Seize_(ARS-26)" class="mw-redirect" title="USS Seize (ARS-26)">USS&#160;<i>Seize</i></a> was brought in to help refloat the ship but it too was grounded. Finally, in January 1945, the <a href="/wiki/USS_Viking_(ARS-1)" title="USS Viking (ARS-1)">USS&#160;<i>Viking</i></a> and <a href="/wiki/USS_Tenino_(ATF-115)" class="mw-redirect" title="USS Tenino (ATF-115)">USS&#160;<i>Tenino</i></a> were able to free the <i>Seize</i> and to offload equipment from <i>LST-563</i> before it was abandoned.<sup id="cite_ref-Lowry1962_176-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lowry1962-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Once the weather station was completed and sailors garrisoned on the island, the U.S. government informed the British, French, and Mexican governments of the station and its purpose.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946785–787_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946785–787-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> Every day at 9 a.m., the 24 sailors stationed at the Clipperton weather station sent up weather balloons to gather information.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFluckey200417_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFluckey200417-181">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFluckey201216_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFluckey201216-182">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> Later, Clipperton was considered for an airfield to shift traffic between North America and Australia far from the front lines of <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Theater_(World_War_II)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pacific Theater (World War II)">Pacific Theater</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 1943, during a meeting between presidents <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Roosevelt</a> of the U.S. and <a href="/wiki/Manuel_%C3%81vila_Camacho" title="Manuel Ávila Camacho">Avila Camacho</a> of Mexico, the topic of Mexican ownership of Clipperton was raised. The American government seemed interested in Clipperton being handed over to Mexico due to the importance the island might play in both commercial and military air travel,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConverse200577–78_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConverse200577–78-184">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> as well as its proximity to the <a href="/wiki/Panama_Canal" title="Panama Canal">Panama Canal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946783_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946783-185">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorne1978262_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorne1978262-186">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConverse2005107_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConverse2005107-188">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although these talks were informal, the U.S. backed away from any Mexican claim on Clipperton as Mexico had previously accepted the 1931 arbitration decision. The U.S. government also felt it would be easier to obtain a military base on the island from France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946786–787_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946786–787-189">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConverse200581_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConverse200581-190">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> However, after the French government was notified about the weather station, relations on this matter deteriorated rapidly<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> with the French government sending a formal note of protest in defense of French sovereignty.<sup id="cite_ref-Thorne1978_178-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thorne1978-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FrenchShark_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FrenchShark-192">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConverse200580–81_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConverse200580–81-193">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> In response, the U.S. extended an offer for the French military to operate the station or to have the Americans agree to leave the weather station under the same framework previously agreed to with other weather stations in France and North Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-French1998_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-French1998-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> There were additional concern within the newly formed <a href="/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_the_French_Republic" title="Provisional Government of the French Republic">Provisional Government of the French Republic</a> that notification of the installation was made to military and not civilian leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrench_Dipolomatic_Documents1998722_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrench_Dipolomatic_Documents1998722-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><span><video id="mwe_player_0" poster="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg/220px--Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg.jpg" controls="" preload="none" class="mw-file-element" width="220" height="124" data-durationhint="124" data-mwtitle="Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons" resource="/wiki/File:Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/3/35/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg.480p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth VP9 (180P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 180P" data-transcodekey="180p.vp9.webm" data-width="320" data-height="180" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/3/35/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg.240p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Small WebM (240P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.webm" data-width="426" data-height="240" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/3/35/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg.360p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="VP9 (360P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 360P" data-transcodekey="360p.vp9.webm" data-width="640" data-height="360" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/3/35/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg/Clipperton_Island_Jeep_and_Radio.ogg.360p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="WebM (360P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 360P" data-transcodekey="360p.webm" data-width="640" data-height="360" /></video></span><figcaption>Video of the crew of the <a href="/wiki/USS_Concord_(CL-10)" title="USS Concord (CL-10)">USS&#160;<i>Concord</i></a> on Clipperton Island landing a Jeep and using a radio</figcaption></figure>
<p>French Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Georges_Bidault" title="Georges Bidault">Georges Bidault</a> said of the incident: "This is very humiliating to us we are anxious to cooperate with you, but sometimes you do not make it easy".<sup id="cite_ref-Thorne1978_178-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thorne1978-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946789_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946789-196">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConverse200580_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConverse200580-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> French Vice Admiral <a href="/wiki/Raymond_Fenard" title="Raymond Fenard">Raymond Fenard</a> requested during a meeting with U.S. Admiral <a href="/wiki/Lyal_A._Davidson" title="Lyal A. Davidson">Lyal A. Davidson</a> that civilians be given access to Clipperton and the surrounding waters,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946788_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946788-198">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> but the U.S. Navy denied the request because there was an active military installation on the island. Instead Davidson offered to transport a French officer to the installation and reassured the French government that the United States did not wish to claim sovereignty over the island.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946791_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946791-199">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> During these discussions between the admirals, French diplomats in Mexico attempted to hire the Mexican vessel <i>Pez de Plata</i> out of <a href="/wiki/Acapulco" title="Acapulco">Acapulco</a> to bring a military attaché to Clipperton under a cover story that they were going on a shark fishing trip.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEElsey194512_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElsey194512-200">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> At the request of the Americans, the Mexican government refused to allow the <i>Pez De Plata</i> to leave port.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946789_196-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946789-196">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> French officials then attempted to leave in another smaller vessel and filed a false destination with the local port authorities but were also stopped by Mexican officials.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEElsey194513_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElsey194513-201">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During this period, French officials in Mexico leaked information about their concerns, as well as about the arrival of seaplanes at Clipperton, to <i>The New York Times</i> and <i>Newsweek</i>; both stories were refused publishing clearance on national security grounds.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEElsey194513–15_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElsey194513–15-202">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> In February 1945, the U.S. Navy transported French Officer Lieutenant Louis Jampierre to Clipperton out of San Diego<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> where he visited the installation and that afternoon returned to the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946792_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946792-204">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> As the war in the Pacific progressed, concerns about Japanese incursions into the Eastern Pacific were reduced and in September 1945 the U.S. Navy began removing from Clipperton.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946794_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiplomatic_Papers1946794-206">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:3_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-207">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> During the evacuation, munitions were destroyed, but significant matériel was left on the island.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_207-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-207">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:4_119-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> By 21 October 1945, the last U.S. Navy staff at the weather station left Clipperton.<sup id="cite_ref-Lowry1962_176-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lowry1962-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-World_War_II_developments">Post-World War II developments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clipperton_Island&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Post-World War II developments">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Since the island was abandoned by American forces at the end of <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, the island has been visited by sports fishermen, French naval patrols, and Mexican tuna and shark fishermen. There have been infrequent scientific and amateur radio expeditions and, in 1978, <a href="/wiki/Jacques-Yves_Cousteau" class="mw-redirect" title="Jacques-Yves Cousteau">Jacques-Yves Cousteau</a> visited with a team of divers and a survivor from the 1917 evacuation to film a television special called <i>Clipperton: The Island that Time Forgot</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The island was visited by ornithologist <a href="/wiki/Kenneth_E._Stager" title="Kenneth E. Stager">Ken Stager</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Natural_History_Museum_of_Los_Angeles_County" title="Natural History Museum of Los Angeles County">Los Angeles County Museum</a> in 1958. Appalled at the depredations visited by feral pigs upon the island's <a href="/wiki/Brown_booby" title="Brown booby">brown booby</a> and <a href="/wiki/Masked_booby" title="Masked booby">masked booby</a> colonies (reduced to 500 and 150&#160;birds, respectively), Stager procured a shotgun and killed all 58&#160;pigs.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> By 2003, the booby colonies had grown to 25,000&#160;brown boobies and 112,000&#160;masked boobies, making Clipperton home to the world's second-largest brown booby colony, and its largest masked booby colony.<sup id="cite_ref-Pitman_et_al._2005_73-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pitman_et_al._2005-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> In 1994, Stager's story inspired Bernie Tershy and Don Croll, both professors at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_California,_Santa_Cruz" title="University of California, Santa Cruz">University of California, Santa Cruz</a> <a href="/wiki/Long_Marine_Laboratory" class="mw-redirect" title="Long Marine Laboratory">Long Marine Lab</a>, to found the non-profit <a href="/wiki/Island_Conservation" title="Island Conservation">Island Conservation</a>, which works to prevent extensions through the removal of invasive species from islands.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When the <a href="/wiki/Independence_of_Algeria" class="mw-redirect" title="Independence of Algeria">independence of Algeria</a> in 1962 threatened <a href="/wiki/French_nuclear_testing" class="mw-redirect" title="French nuclear testing">French nuclear testing</a> sites in North Africa, the <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_(France)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ministry of Defence (France)">French Ministry of Defence</a> considered Clipperton as a possible replacement site. This was eventually ruled out due to the island's hostile climate and remote location, but the island was used to house a small scientific mission to collect data on nuclear fallout from other nuclear tests.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_119-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> From 1966 to 1969, the French military sent a series of missions, called "Bougainville," to the island. The Bougainville missions unloaded some 25 tons of equipment, including sanitary facilities, traditional Polynesian dwellings, drinking water treatment tanks, and generators. The missions sought to surveil the island and its surrounding waters, observe weather conditions, and evaluate potential rehabilitation of the World War II era airstrip.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> By 1978, the structures built during the Bougainville missions had become quite derelict.<sup id="cite_ref-Sacotte1978_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sacotte1978-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The French explored reopening the lagoon and developing a harbour for trade and tourism during the 1970s, but this too was abandoned.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> An automatic weather installation was completed on 7&#160;April 1980, with data collected by the station transmitted via satellite to <a href="/wiki/Brittany" title="Brittany">Brittany</a>.
</p><p>In 1981, the <a href="/wiki/Acad%C3%A9mie_des_sciences_d%27outre-mer" title="Académie des sciences d&#39;outre-mer">Académie des sciences d'outre-mer</a> recommended the island have its own economic infrastructure, with an <a href="/wiki/Airstrip" class="mw-redirect" title="Airstrip">airstrip</a> and a fishing port in the lagoon. This would mean opening the lagoon to the ocean by creating a passage in the atoll rim. To oversee this, the French government reassigned Clipperton from the High Commissioner for French Polynesia to the direct authority of the French government, classifying the island as an overseas state private property administered by France's Overseas Minister. In 1986, the Company for the Study, Development and Exploitation of Clipperton Island (French acronym, <a href="/w/index.php?title=SEDEIC&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="SEDEIC (page does not exist)">SEDEIC</a>) and French officials began outlining a plan for the development of Clipperton as a fishing port,<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> but due to economic constraints, the distance from markets, and the small size of the atoll, nothing beyond preliminary studies was undertaken and plans for the development were abandoned. In the mid-1980s, the French government began efforts to enlist citizens of French Polynesia to settle on Clipperton; these plans were ultimately abandoned as well.<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1994, the <a href="/wiki/CNES" title="CNES">French Space Agency</a> requested the help of <a href="/wiki/NASA" title="NASA">NASA</a> to track the <a href="/wiki/Multistage_rocket" title="Multistage rocket">first stage breakup</a> of the newly designed <a href="/wiki/Ariane_5" title="Ariane 5">Ariane 5</a> rocket.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-215">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> After spending a month on Clipperton setting up and calibrating radar equipment to monitor <a href="/wiki/Ariane_flight_V88" title="Ariane flight V88">Ariane flight V88</a>, the mission ended in disappointment when the rocket disintegrated 37 seconds after launch due to a software bug.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_215-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-215">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Castaways">Castaways</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clipperton_Island&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Castaways">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In May 1893, Charles Jensen and "Brick" Thurman of the Oceanic Phosphate Company were left on the island by the company's ship <i>Compeer</i> with 90 days worth of supplies in order to prevent other attempts to claim the island and its guano. Before sailing for Clipperton, Jensen wrote a letter to the Secretary of the Coast Seamen's Union, Andrew Furuseth, instructing him that if the Oceanic Phosphate Company had not sent a vessel to Clipperton six weeks after the return of the <i>Compeer</i> to make it known that they had been stranded there.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> The Oceanic Phosphate Company denied it had left the men without adequate supplies and contracted the schooner <i>Viking</i> to retrieve them in late August.<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Viking</i> rescued the men, who had used seabirds' eggs to supplement their supplies, and returned them to San Francisco on 31 October.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 1897, the British cargo vessel <i>Kinkora</i> wrecked on Clipperton;<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> the crew was able to salvage food and water from the ship, allowing them to survive on the island in relative comfort. During the crew's time on the island, a passing vessel offered to take the men to the mainland for $1,500, which the crew refused. Instead eight of the men loaded up a lifeboat and rowed to Acapulco for help.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Coolgardie1898_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Coolgardie1898-222">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> After the first mate of the <i>Kinkora</i>, Mr. McMarty, arrived in Acapulco, <a href="/wiki/HMS_Comus_(1878)" title="HMS Comus (1878)">HMS <i>Comus</i></a> set sail from British Columbia to rescue the sailors.<sup id="cite_ref-Coolgardie1898_222-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Coolgardie1898-222">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1947, five American fishermen from San Pedro, California, were rescued from Clipperton after surviving on the island for six weeks.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In early 1962, the island provided a home to nine crewmen of the sunken tuna clipper MV <i>Monarch</i>, stranded for 23 days from 6 February to 1 March.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> They reported that the lagoon water was drinkable, although they preferred to drink water from the coconuts they found. Unable to use any of the dilapidated buildings, they constructed a crude shelter from cement bags and tin salvaged from <a href="/wiki/Quonset_hut" title="Quonset hut">Quonset huts</a> built by the American military 20 years earlier. Wood from the huts was used for firewood, and fish caught off the fringing reef combined with potatoes and onions they had saved from their sinking vessel augmented the island's meager supply of coconuts. The crewmen reported they tried eating bird's eggs, but found them to be rancid, and they decided after trying to cook a 'little black bird' that it did not have enough meat to make the effort worthwhile. Pigs had been eradicated, but the crewmen reported seeing their skeletons around the atoll.<sup id="cite_ref-No94_58-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-No94-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The crewmen were eventually discovered by another fishing boat, and rescued by the U.S. Navy destroyer <a href="/wiki/USS_Robison_(DDG-12)" class="mw-redirect" title="USS Robison (DDG-12)">USS&#160;<i>Robison</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Amateur_radio_DX-peditions">Amateur radio DX-peditions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clipperton_Island&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Amateur radio DX-peditions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clipperton has long been an attractive destination for <a href="/wiki/Amateur_radio" title="Amateur radio">amateur radio</a> groups due to its remoteness, permit requirements, history, and interesting environment. While some radio operation has been part of other visits to the island, major <a href="/wiki/DX-pedition" title="DX-pedition">DX-peditions</a> have included <a href="/wiki/FO0XB_Clipperton_Island_DXpedition" title="FO0XB Clipperton Island DXpedition">FO0XB (1978)</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Sacotte1978_21-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sacotte1978-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> FO0XX (1985), FO0CI (1992), FO0AAA (2000), and TX5C (2008).
</p><p>In March 2014, the Cordell Expedition, organised and led by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Schmieder" title="Robert Schmieder">Robert Schmieder</a>, combined a radio DX-pedition using <a href="/wiki/Callsign" class="mw-redirect" title="Callsign">callsign</a> TX5K with environmental and scientific investigations.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> The team of 24&#160;radio operators made more than 114,000&#160;contacts, breaking the previous record of 75,000. The activity included extensive operation in the <a href="/wiki/6-meter_band" title="6-meter band">6-meter band</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Earth%E2%80%93Moon%E2%80%93Earth_communication" title="Earth–Moon–Earth communication">Earth–Moon–Earth communication</a> (EME) or 'moonbounce' contacts. A notable accomplishment was the use of DXA, a real-time satellite-based online graphic radio log web page, allowing anyone with a browser to see the radio activity. Scientific work conducted during the expedition included the first collection and identification of <a href="/wiki/Foraminifera" title="Foraminifera">foraminifera</a> and extensive aerial imaging of the island using kite-borne cameras. The team included two scientists from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Tahiti" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Tahiti">University of Tahiti</a> and a French TV documentary crew from <a href="/wiki/Thalassa_(TV_series)" title="Thalassa (TV series)"><i>Thalassa</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2015, Alain Duchauchoy, F6BFH, broadcast from Clipperton using callsign TX5P as part of the Passion 2015 scientific expedition to Clipperton Island. Duchauchoy also researched Mexican use of the island during the early 1900s as part of the expedition.<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
